The chapter's examples show that useful combinations often arise when someone notices a neglected customer, connects two distant mechanisms, or questions the customary purpose of a product. Organizational routines should make those observations visible and actionable.
Study instruction:
- "Propose one recurring practice for sharing cross-functional ideas and one control for evaluating them responsibly."
Key point:
- Recombination improves when useful ideas can move across organizational boundaries.
- A supportive culture needs both permission to explore and discipline to test.
53. Q: How can organizations recognize when an existing product or project should be pivoted?
A: Show answer
Organizations should look for repeated evidence that the current offering is not solving the intended problem or that customers assign greater value to a different feature, use, or audience. Signals can include persistent failure to meet outcomes, recurring workarounds, unexpected side effects, customer questions about a secondary feature, or operational costs that remain high despite attempted fixes. No single signal automatically justifies a pivot. Leaders should examine evidence reliability, the value of capabilities already developed, and the feasibility of alternatives. Wrigley's changing products and Brown's redirected research show how a different opportunity can emerge from the same underlying effort. A decision to pivot should be documented and reviewed against agreed measures.
Study instruction:
- "Define three early-warning signals for a project and specify what evidence would trigger a formal review of its direction."
Key point:
- Repeated patterns are more informative than isolated surprises.
- Pivot decisions should be based on evidence, opportunity, and transferable capabilities.
54. Q: How can customer feedback become a strategic decision rather than a collection of anecdotes?
A: Show answer
Customer comments become strategically useful when organized into patterns and compared with observed behavior. Teams should record who provided feedback, the situation in which the problem occurred, how frequently it appears, and what the customer did to work around it. They should compare observations with usage, sales, retention, service requests, and other relevant evidence. The chapter's examples show that customer response can reveal a product or feature whose value exceeds its original role. However, an enthusiastic comment from one person is not enough to justify a major pivot. The team should determine whether the signal recurs across a meaningful segment and whether the proposed change improves an outcome customers value.
Study instruction:
- "Create a feedback matrix grouping observations by problem, user segment, frequency, workaround, and potential business implication."
Key point:
- Anecdotes become stronger evidence when recurring patterns are verified.
- Strategic decisions should connect customer feedback with behavior and measurable outcomes.
55. Q: How can a creator decide which parts of an idea to preserve and which to change?
A: Show answer
The small-bird metaphor offers a practical decision rule: hold tightly to the essential purpose or value, but gently to the particular form used to deliver it. A team should identify the problem it is committed to solving, the users it intends to help, and the principles that must not be compromised. It can then examine whether product features, assumptions, audience, process, or distribution are merely one possible way to achieve that purpose. Brown remained a chemist while redirecting a drug's application; Wrigley changed products while retaining an understanding of customer incentives. This distinction prevents both rigid attachment and unnecessary abandonment. It also makes a pivot easier to explain because the team can show what remains continuous.
Study instruction:
- "For one idea, write two lists: non-negotiable purpose or safeguards, and changeable methods or assumptions."
Key point:
- Preserve the underlying value while reconsidering the means.
- A clear distinction between fixed principles and flexible methods supports purposeful change.
56. Q: What is the relationship between recombination, pivoting, and learning from failure?
A: Show answer
Recombination and pivoting both depend on learning from what already exists. Recombination searches for new relationships among known elements; pivoting uses evidence from an unsuccessful or unexpected result to redirect an effort. Failure can supply information about the limits of an approach, the needs of users, or the presence of an alternative opportunity. The chapter's examples show that a disappointing outcome is not automatically worthless, but neither is it automatically a hidden success. It must be examined carefully. The useful response is to identify what was learned, what can be retained, what should change, and what evidence is needed next. Learning converts failure from a final verdict into input for a more informed decision.
Study instruction:
- "After a setback, document the original assumption, observed result, retained learning, possible recombination, and possible pivot."
Key point:
- Failure can provide information for a new combination or direction.
- Learning requires interpreting evidence, not merely relabeling a setback as success.
57. Q: How can a professional build a repeatable personal practice for getting unstuck?
A: Show answer
A repeatable practice can combine an idea journal, deliberate exposure to different fields, a willingness to produce imperfect first attempts, and structured review when progress stalls. The person can define the obstacle, search the journal for ideas that may connect to it, and question assumptions about how the problem must be solved. If an attempt fails, the person can ask what remains useful and what moderate change might improve the result. A short experiment can then test the most promising alternative. The practice should include a stopping rule so exploration does not become endless. The chapter's examples suggest that creativity can be supported by habits that preserve observations and make adaptation routine.
Study instruction:
- "Create a weekly routine for capturing ideas, reviewing one difficult problem, generating combinations, and testing one small change."
Key point:
- Getting unstuck can be supported by repeatable habits rather than waiting for inspiration.
- Capture, connect, test, and review ideas in a deliberate cycle.
58. Q: How should a team decide whether to continue, modify, scale, or stop an experiment?
A: Show answer
The decision should follow evidence collected against the experiment's original objective. Continue when the test remains informative and risks are acceptable; modify when results reveal a correctable weakness or important new assumption; scale when the benefit is repeatable and the organization can support wider use; and stop when evidence shows that the idea is unsafe, impractical, or unlikely to create sufficient value. The chapter's cases emphasize listening to unexpected results and choosing among alternatives rather than persisting automatically. Teams should record the decision, evidence considered, remaining uncertainty, and next review point. This creates a record of learning and reduces the chance that enthusiasm or sunk costs will determine the outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Write decision rules for continuing, modifying, scaling, and stopping a pilot before its results are known."
Key point:
- Experiment decisions should be tied to predefined evidence and acceptable risk.
- Documenting the reasoning makes learning reusable and decisions accountable.
59. Q: How can teams use diverse perspectives without allowing discussion to become unfocused?
A: Show answer
Diverse perspectives are useful when the team gives them a shared problem and a clear way to contribute. Members should agree on the outcome being pursued, invite different interpretations and analogies, group ideas by the problem they address, test assumptions, and use explicit criteria to compare options. This prevents diversity from becoming a collection of unrelated opinions. The chapter's emphasis on distributing expertise suggests that no single participant needs to hold all the knowledge, but the team must create a process for integrating what each person knows. Clear roles, respectful challenge, and documented decisions help preserve openness and accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Facilitate a cross-functional review using one shared problem statement, independent idea generation, and agreed evaluation criteria."
Key point:
- Diversity becomes useful when different knowledge is connected to a common problem.
- Structure the decision process without restricting the range of ideas considered.
60. Q: How can a recombination be evaluated for usefulness rather than novelty alone?
A: Show answer
A recombination should be assessed by whether it solves a real problem better than available alternatives. The team should identify the intended user, the need being addressed, the benefit created by combining the components, and the costs or risks introduced. It should ask whether the new arrangement is understandable, feasible, accessible, and sustainable. A concept that looks surprising but does not improve an outcome may be interesting without being useful. The Jitterbug example demonstrates the importance of customer fit, while the alarm-mat example demonstrates the importance of a practical mechanism. Evaluation should include user feedback and real-world testing rather than relying solely on the creator's excitement.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a proposed combination for user need, mechanism, benefit, feasibility, risk, and test evidence."
Key point:
- Novelty is a characteristic; usefulness is a result that must be demonstrated.
- Evaluate a recombination against the problem and the people it is meant to serve.
61. Q: What is the difference between changing a product and changing its market position?
A: Show answer
Changing a product alters its features, design, service, or capabilities. Changing market position alters the audience, use case, value proposition, or context in which the product is offered. The Jitterbug case combines both: a familiar flip-phone form was adapted with large buttons, a bright display, and personal support for older adults. Wrigley's gum story also shows how a product's role can change when customers begin to seek it out as a regular item rather than accept it as a bonus. A pivot may involve one or both dimensions. Teams should specify what is changing because product changes need usability and performance testing, while positioning changes need evidence about customer needs and adoption.
Study instruction:
- "For a product you know, propose one feature change and one market-position change, then identify how each would be tested."
Key point:
- A pivot can change what a product does, who it serves, or how customers understand its value.
- Define the dimension of change so the right evidence can be collected.
62. Q: How can a creator avoid confusing a promising analogy with a superficial resemblance?
A: Show answer
A useful analogy identifies a shared causal mechanism, not merely a similar appearance. VanMoof's television-box idea was relevant because both products traveled in similarly shaped packages and the television image could signal fragility to handlers. A superficial analogy would copy the look of another product without explaining why the change should affect the problem. To evaluate an analogy, ask what behavior or process it is expected to change, why that mechanism should transfer, which contextual differences could weaken it, and how the result will be measured. Analogies are tools for generating hypotheses. They become reliable only when the proposed mechanism is tested in the new environment.
Study instruction:
- "For an analogy you are considering, state the shared mechanism, important contextual differences, and a test that could disprove it."
Key point:
- A strong analogy transfers a mechanism, not just a visual similarity.
- Test whether the borrowed principle works under the new context's constraints.
63. Q: Why should a pivot preserve useful capabilities instead of discarding the entire past effort?
A: Show answer
Earlier work may contain valuable skills, relationships, technology, customer knowledge, or infrastructure even when the original objective is no longer viable. Brown's team retained its chemistry knowledge while exploring a different medical application. Wrigley retained his ability to understand incentives and sell consumer products while moving from soap to baking powder and gum. Preserving useful capabilities reduces the cost of changing direction and allows learning to compound. However, a capability should be retained because it contributes to the new objective, not simply because the organization has invested in it. A disciplined pivot distinguishes transferable strengths from sunk costs. This prevents both wasteful abandonment and continued use of assets that no longer fit.
Study instruction:
- "List the capabilities developed in a stalled project and classify each as transferable, irrelevant, or costly to retain under a proposed new direction."
Key point:
- A pivot should reuse capabilities that genuinely support the new objective.
- Past investment alone is not a reason to preserve an unsuitable activity.
64. Q: How can people avoid being trapped by sunk costs when deciding whether to pivot?
A: Show answer
Sunk costs are resources already spent that cannot be recovered simply by continuing the same plan. The chapter implies that a team should evaluate the current opportunity rather than defend an earlier decision because of the time, money, or reputation invested in it. A practical review asks what additional resources are required from today onward, what outcome is realistically expected, and whether another path offers better value. It should distinguish emotional attachment from evidence of future benefit. The effort already spent may have produced transferable knowledge, even if the original project stops. Treating that learning as an asset helps people change direction without pretending the earlier investment was pointless.
Study instruction:
- "Reassess a project using future costs, expected future benefits, and transferable learning; record sunk costs separately."
Key point:
- Past expenditure cannot justify future expenditure by itself.
- Retain the learning from an effort without being bound to its original plan.
65. Q: What role does curiosity play in recognizing opportunities hidden inside setbacks?
A: Show answer
Curiosity keeps attention open when an outcome does not match expectations. Brown's decisive moment came when he asked trial participants whether they had noticed anything else, rather than ending the conversation when the original heart-drug objective appeared unsuccessful. Curiosity can reveal side effects, unmet needs, workarounds, or patterns that were not part of the initial hypothesis. It should be paired with careful listening and verification; otherwise, unusual observations can be overinterpreted. In practical work, curiosity means asking follow-up questions, inviting unexpected feedback, and resisting the urge to explain away inconvenient evidence. It turns a setback into an occasion for learning and may expose a more promising question than the one originally posed.
Study instruction:
- "Write five neutral follow-up questions you could ask when a test or project produces an unexpected result."
Key point:
- Curiosity can uncover information that the original plan was not designed to collect.
- Listen first, then verify before converting an observation into a new direction.
66. Q: How can people maintain emotional control when an important project appears close to failure?
A: Show answer
The chapter portrays Brown's team as demoralized and close to being shut down. Brown's ability to remain engaged long enough to ask an additional question was essential. Emotional control does not mean ignoring disappointment or pretending that a project is succeeding. It means preventing frustration, embarrassment, or fear from ending inquiry before the evidence has been examined. A team can support this by separating the review of results from blame, recording what has been learned, and creating a short, structured period for alternative explanations. Leaders should also be realistic about deadlines and resource limits. Emotional steadiness protects judgment at the moment when people may be most tempted to react defensively.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a setback-review routine that separates emotional reactions, observed facts, possible explanations, and next decisions."
Key point:
- Disappointment can narrow attention if it is allowed to end inquiry prematurely.
- A calm review protects curiosity and decision quality without denying the seriousness of failure.
67. Q: How can a person tell whether to recombine ideas or pivot the current approach?
A: Show answer
Recombination is especially useful when the problem remains meaningful but the available ideas or components have not been connected in a productive way. Pivoting is more appropriate when evidence suggests that the current direction, application, audience, or assumption is not delivering the intended result. The two approaches can also work together: a pivot may be created by recombining a capability with a new use or customer group. The decision begins with diagnosing the obstacle. If the obstacle is a missing connection, explore combinations. If it is a flawed direction or unmet assumption, consider changing course. In either case, test the proposal and preserve the elements that continue to create value.
Study instruction:
- "Diagnose a stalled effort and explain whether its main obstacle calls for a new combination, a change of direction, or both."
Key point:
- Recombination changes relationships among elements; pivoting changes the direction or application of effort.
- The right response depends on the cause of the obstacle, not a preferred innovation method.
68. Q: What overall principles connect the chapter's examples of Dylan, Harris, Brown, Wrigley, and VanMoof?
A: Show answer
The examples share a willingness to look beyond the obvious interpretation of an idea or result. Dylan and Harris recombined familiar elements for a distinctive creative or customer purpose. Brown and Wrigley noticed that an unexpected result or secondary product could point toward a more valuable direction. VanMoof borrowed a behavioral cue from another context to solve a shipping problem. Each case involved attention to evidence, openness to alternatives, and a willingness to change the form of an idea without losing sight of the value being pursued. The chapter also emphasizes that breakthroughs are rarely the product of isolated genius alone; they depend on influences, customers, collaborators, and accumulated knowledge.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the five examples by identifying the original element, overlooked signal or connection, change made, and value created."
Key point:
- Breakthroughs often emerge from noticing, combining, and adapting what already exists.
- Creative progress depends on openness, evidence, and collaboration as well as individual insight.
Rows: 68
Table 9
Diversity, Networks, and Crowdsourcing for Breakthroughs
1. Q: How can diversity and crowdsourcing help people and teams get unstuck?
A: Show answer
The central argument is that people often become stuck when familiar habits, assumptions, and relationships repeatedly direct them toward the same solutions. Diversity introduces perspectives, experiences, knowledge, and problem-solving methods that are not already represented in the immediate group. Crowdsourcing extends this principle by inviting contributions from a wider population, while an internal crowd uses deliberate perspective-taking to challenge one's own first judgment. These approaches are especially useful when a problem is complex, unfamiliar, or resistant to established routines. Their purpose is not to collect the largest possible number of opinions; it is to obtain meaningfully different information, test alternatives, and discover options that a closed group may overlook. Different input must still be assessed against evidence and practical constraints before it is adopted.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how an outsider, a diverse team, an online crowd, and a deliberate second perspective could each help with a problem that has resisted routine solutions."
Key point:
- Different perspectives can interrupt entrenched habits and reveal options that a familiar group may miss.
- The value comes from useful differences followed by disciplined evaluation, not from variety or volume alone.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
2. Q: Why do people rely on habits and routines, and how can those habits become obstacles?
A: Show answer
Habits reduce the cognitive effort required for recurring decisions and activities. Once a person has learned a reliable way to choose, move, work, or respond, the brain can perform parts of the activity with limited conscious attention. This conserves time and mental resources, but it also encourages automatic repetition. A routine that is efficient in familiar conditions may become unsuitable when the environment, task, or evidence changes. The difficulty is not that habits are inherently harmful; it is that people may continue using them after the conditions that made them useful have disappeared. A team can therefore keep repeating a familiar process even when delays, quality problems, or changing customer needs show that the process is no longer adequate. Recognizing a rut requires noticing repeated outcomes rather than assuming that familiarity proves effectiveness.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a routine that saves effort in normal conditions and specify the warning signs that would indicate it needs to be reconsidered."
Key point:
- Habits provide efficiency by reducing repeated deliberation.
- A useful routine becomes a liability when it is applied automatically to changed circumstances.
3. Q: What is a creative or operational rut, and how can it be recognized?
A: Show answer
A rut is a pattern in which an individual or group repeatedly uses familiar approaches but makes little meaningful progress. It can appear as recurring project delays, repeated failed experiments, persistent disagreement, declining audience interest, or a succession of solutions that address symptoms without changing the underlying problem. A rut is not simply a period of difficulty: complex work can involve setbacks while still generating learning. The stronger warning sign is repetition without useful adaptation. Teams should examine whether the same assumptions, participants, decision rules, or information sources keep producing the same outcome. They should also distinguish a temporary implementation problem from a deeper problem-solving pattern. Once a rut is recognized, the next step is to introduce a new perspective or test a different approach rather than merely increasing effort on the existing one.
Study instruction:
- "Review a stalled project and list the repeated assumptions, actions, and outcomes that suggest a genuine rut rather than a temporary setback."
Key point:
- Repeated effort is not evidence of progress when the underlying approach never changes.
- A rut is often exposed by recurring outcomes and an absence of learning or adaptation.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
4. Q: What does network nonredundancy mean in the context of creativity?
A: Show answer
Network nonredundancy describes the extent to which a person's collaborators provide access to different people, experiences, knowledge, or social circles rather than repeatedly supplying the same information. A network is more nonredundant when its members connect the focal person to perspectives that are not already available through existing relationships. The concept is not simply the number of contacts a person has. A large network can still be highly redundant if everyone shares similar training, assumptions, and connections. A smaller network may be more useful if it bridges otherwise separate professional or intellectual communities. Nonredundant relationships can expose a person to unfamiliar information and alternative ways of framing a problem, increasing the chance that existing knowledge will be recombined in a novel way.
Study instruction:
- "Map a professional network and identify which contacts provide genuinely different expertise, communities, or information."
Key point:
- Network value depends partly on the distinctiveness of what collaborators contribute.
- More contacts do not necessarily mean more nonredundant knowledge.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
5. Q: How does a closed network differ from an open or nonredundant network?
A: Show answer
A closed network is one in which collaborators are strongly connected to one another and tend to share established practices, relationships, and information. Such a network can be valuable for trust, coordination, rapid communication, and execution because participants understand one another's working styles. Its limitation is that the same information and assumptions may circulate repeatedly, reducing exposure to unfamiliar ideas. An open or nonredundant network connects a person to collaborators who bring different relationships or knowledge. It can widen the range of available perspectives, but it may also require more time to establish trust and coordinate work. The appropriate design depends on the task: stable, familiar relationships may support routine execution, whereas difficult or novel problems often benefit from access to people outside the usual circle.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a stable team and a cross-boundary team, identifying the task conditions under which each structure is likely to be useful."
Key point:
- Closed networks can support coordination but may reinforce shared assumptions.
- Open networks can broaden knowledge, although their benefits depend on effective collaboration and task context.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
6. Q: What did research on the creative teams behind Doctor Who reveal about collaboration and novelty?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes research examining the producers, directors, and writers who worked on Doctor Who over several decades. The underlying study analyzed creative contributions by 200 artists across 233 episodes and examined how collaboration networks changed over time. Its central finding was not simply that open networks are always better. Open networks and exposure to heterogeneous content supported creativity more strongly when collaborators and relationships were renewed rather than remaining stable. New ties could disrupt rigid routines and change how existing knowledge was organized and used. The case illustrates how a long-running creative product can benefit from bringing in people who have worked in different circles. It does not prove that every new collaborator will improve every project; the study concerned a specific creative industry and its project-based production structure.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the Doctor Who study's sample, the meaning of network stability, and why its findings should not be generalized without considering the setting."
Key point:
- Network renewal helped creators use nonredundant knowledge more creatively over time.
- Research findings must be interpreted in light of the industry, task, and team structure studied.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
7. Q: Why can adding new collaborators matter even when a team already has access to diverse knowledge?
A: Show answer
Access to diverse knowledge does not guarantee that a team will use it. Over time, stable relationships can produce familiar interaction patterns, predictable roles, and routines for deciding which ideas deserve attention. These patterns may limit the creative value of information that is technically available. New collaborators can interrupt those routines by asking different questions, challenging unspoken assumptions, and changing how participants exchange and combine knowledge. The Doctor Who study described in the chapter suggests that the creative benefits of open networks are stronger when network composition is renewed. Managers should therefore consider both who is connected to the team and whether the team periodically develops new working relationships. Renewal should be purposeful rather than constant turnover, because excessive change can undermine trust, continuity, and delivery.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one knowledge gap and one relationship pattern in a team, then propose a targeted way to introduce a new collaborator without losing essential continuity."
Key point:
- Diverse information can remain unused when interaction routines are rigid.
- New ties can renew collaboration patterns, but turnover should be balanced against continuity.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
8. Q: Through what mechanisms can new collaborators stimulate creative thinking?
A: Show answer
New collaborators can stimulate creativity through at least two related mechanisms. First, they contribute ideas, experiences, and knowledge that are not already circulating within the team. These inputs can be combined with existing knowledge to create alternatives that no individual would have generated alone. Second, the arrival of someone unfamiliar can disrupt habitual ways of interacting. The team may need to explain assumptions that were previously taken for granted, reconsider its division of work, or test a different sequence of decisions. This can change not only the content of discussion but also the way the group processes information. The benefit is strongest when the new perspective is relevant to the problem and the team is willing to examine it rather than dismissing it as unfamiliar.
Study instruction:
- "For a recent team decision, identify which new information and which changes in interaction could have produced a different outcome."
Key point:
- New people can contribute both different ideas and different ways of working.
- Novel input creates value when the team actively examines and combines it with existing knowledge.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
- Team Creativity and Innovation in Culturally Diverse Teams: A Meta-analysis
9. Q: What does the term "black sheep" mean in the chapter's discussion of innovation?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses "black sheep" to describe contributors whose ideas, experiences, or working styles differ from the prevailing norms of a group. They may be outsiders, dissenters, unconventional specialists, or employees whose suggestions have been overlooked because the established process appears successful. Their value lies in the different perspective they can introduce, not in being difficult for its own sake. A black sheep may reveal a hidden assumption, propose a simpler method, or question whether a long-standing quality standard is necessary for every part of a project. Leaders should create a fair opportunity for unconventional ideas to be tested, while retaining standards for evidence, safety, and accountability. Difference alone does not make a proposal sound; it makes the proposal worth evaluating when the team is stuck.
Study instruction:
- "Identify an unconventional idea that was dismissed in a team and describe how it could be tested fairly against the current approach."
Key point:
- A black sheep can expose assumptions that insiders no longer question.
- Unconventional ideas should be tested, not automatically accepted or rejected.
Source links:
- Innovation Lessons from Pixar: An Interview with Brad Bird
10. Q: Must an outsider be more knowledgeable or more talented than the existing team to be useful?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter discusses experiments suggesting that a contributor can stimulate progress because their behavior or perspective differs from the group's established pattern, even when that contributor is not the most competent person in the task. A less experienced participant may ask a basic question that experts have stopped asking, make an unexpected move, or cause others to explore a strategy they had ignored. This does not mean expertise is unimportant or that poor advice should be followed. It means that the value of a contributor has more than one dimension: technical competence, independence of perspective, and the ability to trigger useful reconsideration. Teams should separate the value of generating alternatives from the authority to approve or implement them.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish idea generation from technical approval and explain how a non-expert can contribute without replacing qualified review."
Key point:
- A different perspective can be useful even when it does not come from the most experienced person.
- Novelty may trigger exploration, while expert review remains necessary for high-consequence decisions.
Source links:
- Innovation Lessons from Pixar: An Interview with Brad Bird
11. Q: How did the noisy-bot experiment illustrate the value of disruption in group problem-solving?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a networked puzzle experiment in which participants could observe one another's moves but could not communicate directly. Some versions included artificial agents that made occasional random or poor decisions. These noisy bots sometimes frustrated the human participants, but their unpredictable behavior prompted people to try alternative strategies. The important mechanism was not that the bots supplied correct solutions; it was that their unusual moves changed the behavior of connected participants and could produce wider effects through the network. This example illustrates how a perturbation can break a group out of an established pattern. In real organizations, however, disruption should be bounded by the task's risk and cost. Deliberate experiments are preferable to uncontrolled interference with safety-critical work.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how an unexpected move can change a team's search strategy and identify a low-risk way to test that mechanism."
Key point:
- A disruptive input can prompt exploration even when it is not itself a solution.
- Experimentation should be controlled so that learning does not create unacceptable operational risk.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
12. Q: What did Brad Bird's work at Pixar demonstrate about bringing outsiders into a successful organization?
A: Show answer
Pixar had achieved major success with Toy Story, A Bug's Life, and Toy Story 2, yet its leaders were concerned that success could create complacency. They invited Brad Bird, whose approach differed from established practices, to challenge the studio's assumptions. Bird sought contributors who had alternative ideas and gave them an opportunity to test those ideas on demanding projects. His approach challenged the belief that every animated image had to be produced in the same highly exacting way. The case shows that an organization can use an outsider to question the habits that helped create past success but may now constrain future work. It also shows that leadership must provide permission, resources, and a credible route for experimentation; merely hiring a different person while preserving every old constraint is unlikely to produce meaningful change.
Study instruction:
- "Review a successful project or unit and identify one assumption that could become a source of complacency if it is never challenged."
Key point:
- Past success can make established methods difficult to question.
- Outsiders need authority and a fair opportunity to test alternatives if their contribution is to matter.
Source links:
- Innovation Lessons from Pixar: An Interview with Brad Bird
13. Q: What is the lesson behind Brad Bird's distinction between perfect, very good, and good-enough animation shots?
A: Show answer
Bird challenged the assumption that every shot deserved the same level of technical refinement. Some images were central to the story and needed exceptional quality; others needed to be very good; and some only needed to be convincing enough not to interrupt the audience's experience. This is a form of differentiated effort allocation. It recognizes that time and resources are limited and that applying the highest possible standard to every component can delay the entire project without proportionate benefit. The principle should not be misused to excuse unsafe, inaccurate, or noncompliant work. Teams should define which elements are critical, which can use simpler methods, and what minimum acceptance criteria apply to each. The correct standard depends on the consequences of failure and the value the component adds to the final result.
Study instruction:
- "Classify the components of a current project by criticality and justify where exceptional quality, normal quality, or a fit-for-purpose standard is appropriate."
Key point:
- Effort should be allocated according to the importance and risk of each component.
- Good-enough standards require explicit acceptance criteria, not arbitrary shortcuts.
Source links:
- Innovation Lessons from Pixar: An Interview with Brad Bird
14. Q: What kinds of diversity can improve the information available to a team?
A: Show answer
Diversity can arise from differences in professional training, functional expertise, experience, education, culture, problem-solving style, networks, lived experience, and demographic background. These forms of difference are related but not interchangeable. A team may be demographically varied while sharing similar technical assumptions, or it may have limited demographic variation but include strongly different professional disciplines. The chapter emphasizes diversity as a source of nonredundant input: people notice different problems, use different evidence, and may interpret the same facts differently. The practical question is therefore not only whether a team appears diverse, but whether it includes the perspectives needed for the task and whether those perspectives can influence decisions. Diversity should be considered in relation to the problem, not treated as a symbolic target detached from work.
Study instruction:
- "List the forms of diversity relevant to a complex decision and identify which perspectives are currently missing from the decision-making group."
Key point:
- Diversity includes differences in knowledge, experience, networks, and demographic background.
- The useful mix depends on the information and judgment the task requires.
Source links:
- Team Creativity and Innovation in Culturally Diverse Teams: A Meta-analysis
15. Q: Why can homogeneous research teams overlook important questions or populations?
A: Show answer
Researchers' backgrounds can influence which problems they notice, which hypotheses they consider plausible, and which populations they include in a study. When a team shares similar experiences, it may fail to recognize that a research question or sample excludes people whose needs differ from its own. The chapter uses the history of medical research to illustrate how male-dominated research teams and male-only participant samples left some women's health questions less visible. The lesson is not that a researcher's identity determines the quality of their work. It is that a narrow range of experiences can create blind spots, so teams should examine who helped frame the question, whose data are included, and who may be affected by the findings. Representation, inclusive recruitment, and methodological review can improve the relevance of research without replacing rigorous design.
Study instruction:
- "Audit a research proposal for gaps in team perspective, sampling, inclusion criteria, and the population to which conclusions will be applied."
Key point:
- Narrow experience can shape research questions and samples in ways that exclude relevant perspectives.
- Inclusive design and transparent sampling improve the credibility and applicability of findings.
Source links:
- Gender-Diverse Teams Produce More Novel and Higher-Impact Scientific Ideas
16. Q: What did the large-scale study of gender-diverse medical research teams find?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses research examining millions of medical publications produced between 2000 and 2019. The published study analyzed approximately 6.6 million papers and found that mixed-gender research teams produced work that was more novel and more likely to be highly cited than comparable single-gender teams. The paper reported that mixed-gender teams' work was up to 7 percent more novel and 14.6 percent more likely to fall among upper-tail papers. These are study-specific associations, not a universal guarantee that any mixed-gender team will outperform any other team. The findings support examining whether research teams draw on a broad range of perspectives, but they do not remove the need to evaluate expertise, methods, sample quality, or the actual scientific contribution of each study.
Study instruction:
- "State the study's population and reported measures, then distinguish an observed association from a universal causal rule."
Key point:
- The study linked gender-diverse medical research teams with greater novelty and research impact.
- Its numerical findings describe a large research sample and should not be converted into a guarantee for individual teams.
Source links:
- Gender-Diverse Teams Produce More Novel and Higher-Impact Scientific Ideas
17. Q: How should numerical findings about diversity and innovation be interpreted responsibly?
A: Show answer
A reported percentage should always be read with its population, comparison group, outcome measure, study design, and limitations. For example, a finding that one type of team publishes more highly cited papers does not establish that demographic composition alone caused the difference, nor does it mean every team will experience the same effect. Researchers may control for some characteristics, but unmeasured factors can remain. Citation impact is also not identical to practical usefulness, scientific accuracy, or ethical quality. A careful interpretation distinguishes association from causation, average effects from individual outcomes, and statistical measures from managerial decisions. The most defensible conclusion is that diversity can be a valuable source of novel information and should be evaluated alongside team processes, expertise, resources, and the nature of the task.
Study instruction:
- "For any diversity statistic, record the sample, comparator, outcome, design, and limitations before using it to support a management decision."
Key point:
- Research percentages are meaningful only when their measurement and context are understood.
- Diversity is one contributor to performance, not a substitute for sound methods or competent execution.
Source links:
- Gender-Diverse Teams Produce More Novel and Higher-Impact Scientific Ideas
- Team Creativity and Innovation in Culturally Diverse Teams: A Meta-analysis
18. Q: How can diversity influence innovation and performance in business teams?
A: Show answer
Diverse teams can improve innovation when their members contribute different information, question assumptions, and combine complementary expertise. A person with experience in customer service may notice a usability problem that engineers overlook, while a finance specialist may identify a cost or sustainability issue that designers have not considered. The benefit comes through information exchange and integration, not merely through assembling people with different characteristics. Research on culturally diverse teams finds that the relationship with creativity depends on the kind of diversity and the task context. Organizations should therefore design work so that differences are discussed and used, rather than assuming that representation automatically produces innovation. They should also monitor whether team members have equal opportunities to contribute and whether decisions reflect the evidence raised by different participants.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a business problem and identify the different functions or experiences needed to generate, evaluate, and implement a solution."
Key point:
- Diversity can widen the information used to develop and assess ideas.
- Innovation depends on how effectively the team integrates those perspectives into action.
Source links:
- Team Creativity and Innovation in Culturally Diverse Teams: A Meta-analysis
- Gender-Diverse Teams Produce More Novel and Higher-Impact Scientific Ideas
19. Q: Under what conditions is diversity especially useful for solving a problem?
A: Show answer
Diversity is particularly useful when the problem is complex, novel, uncertain, or resistant to standard procedures. Such tasks require people to identify multiple causes, evaluate competing explanations, and combine knowledge from different domains. The chapter argues that breakthroughs often have these characteristics because familiar approaches have already failed to resolve the sticking point. A meta-analysis of culturally diverse teams found that the relationship between diversity and creativity varied by the type of diversity, task complexity, interdependence, and team setting. This means diversity should be matched to the task. A simple, repetitive activity may gain little from extensive discussion among a highly varied group, while a difficult design or research problem may benefit substantially from varied expertise and perspectives.
Study instruction:
- "Classify a task by novelty, complexity, uncertainty, and interdependence, then justify the level and type of diversity it requires."
Key point:
- Complex and novel problems often benefit from broader perspectives and knowledge.
- The effect of diversity depends on task characteristics and how the team works together.

20. Q: Why does diversity not automatically produce better teamwork or better decisions?
A: Show answer
Differences can introduce useful information, but they can also create misunderstanding, relationship conflict, coordination costs, or competing priorities. If team members feel excluded or believe that disagreement will be punished, they may withhold the very information that makes diversity valuable. A meta-analysis of culturally diverse teams found that the effects of diversity on creativity and innovation are moderated by task and team conditions; other reviews also emphasize that diversity can operate through both information exchange and social categorization. Leaders should therefore create clear goals, fair participation rules, respectful challenge, and explicit decision procedures. Diversity is not a guarantee of harmony or performance. Its potential is realized when the team can surface disagreement, examine evidence, and integrate different contributions without allowing conflict to become personal or destructive.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a diverse team's performance by reviewing both its information-sharing process and its relationship or coordination problems."
Key point:
- Diversity creates both opportunities for information exchange and risks of social division.
- Effective leadership turns differences into constructive analysis rather than unmanaged conflict.
Source links:
- Team Creativity and Innovation in Culturally Diverse Teams: A Meta-analysis
- It's About the Process, Not the Product: A Meta-analytic Investigation of Team Demographic Diversity and Processes
21. Q: How can a manager make it safe for unconventional contributors to challenge established practices?
A: Show answer
A manager should make the purpose of challenge explicit, invite evidence-based disagreement, and show that people can question an idea without being personally attacked. The Pixar example in the chapter describes a team in which contributors were given permission to test unusual methods, while Brad Bird's interview also emphasizes the importance of morale, open discussion, and learning from one another's strengths. Psychological safety does not mean that every suggestion is accepted or that performance standards disappear. It means that team members can raise concerns, admit uncertainty, and offer alternatives without fear of humiliation or retaliation. Managers should respond to dissent by asking what evidence supports the idea, what risks it creates, and how it could be tested. They should also explain decisions so that rejected proposals are not treated as evidence that speaking up was pointless.
Study instruction:
- "Review a team meeting and identify leader behaviors that either encourage or discourage evidence-based dissent."
Key point:
- Unconventional ideas require a climate in which people can raise and test them.
- Psychological safety supports candor while accountability preserves standards.
Source links:
- Innovation Lessons from Pixar: An Interview with Brad Bird
- Team Creativity and Innovation in Culturally Diverse Teams: A Meta-analysis
22. Q: What did Shane Battier's basketball career illustrate about unconventional contributions to a team?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents Shane Battier as a player whose value was not fully visible in conventional individual statistics. He studied opponents, used detailed information to exploit their tendencies, defended strategically, and often chose actions that improved team performance rather than maximizing his own scoring totals. The example challenges the assumption that a valuable contributor must excel on the most visible measures. A player can create value by making teammates more effective, reducing opponents' advantages, or performing tasks that are not captured by simple box-score statistics. The wider lesson applies beyond sport: organizations should examine how a person's contribution affects the whole system, not only their individual output. At the same time, one player's story is an illustration rather than proof that unconventional style always improves performance.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a role in your organization whose value may be hidden by individual output measures and describe the wider team effect it creates."
Key point:
- A contributor's value may appear in the performance of the whole team rather than in personal totals.
- Unconventional strengths are useful when they complement the capabilities of other members.
Source links:
- The No-Stats All-Star: Michael Lewis's Profile of Shane Battier
23. Q: What does the plus-minus statistic measure, and what are its limitations?
A: Show answer
Plus-minus measures the difference between a team's points scored and points allowed while a particular player is on the court. A positive value indicates that the team outscored its opponent during those minutes; a negative value indicates the opposite. It can reveal contributions that are not obvious from an individual's points, rebounds, or assists. However, a basic plus-minus figure is influenced by teammates, opponents, lineups, game situations, and sample size. It should not be treated as a pure measure of an individual's causal impact. The chapter uses Battier's high plus-minus as an illustration of how team effects can differ from individual statistics. A sound evaluation combines contextual statistics, role analysis, observation, and comparisons across appropriate situations rather than relying on one number.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the meaning of a positive plus-minus score and list contextual factors that can affect its interpretation."
Key point:
- Plus-minus captures team scoring outcomes during a player's time on court.
- It is a contextual indicator, not a standalone measure of individual ability or causation.
Source links:
- The No-Stats All-Star: Michael Lewis's Profile of Shane Battier
24. Q: Why should organizations measure team contribution rather than only individual output?
A: Show answer
Individual output measures are often easier to count, but they can overlook enabling work such as mentoring, coordination, risk detection, knowledge sharing, and removing obstacles for colleagues. A person may appear less productive on a narrow metric because they spend time helping the group avoid errors or complete difficult work. Conversely, high individual output can conceal behavior that creates rework or weakens collaboration. Organizations should therefore use a balanced set of indicators that includes individual deliverables, quality, timeliness, collaboration, customer or beneficiary outcomes, and the performance of the wider team. Measures should be chosen according to the purpose of the role and checked for unintended incentives. The Battier example illustrates the need to look beyond visible personal statistics when evaluating contribution to collective performance.
Study instruction:
- "Design a role scorecard that combines individual results with measurable indicators of collaboration and team outcomes."
Key point:
- Narrow individual metrics can miss contributions that improve collective performance.
- Balanced measurement reduces the risk of rewarding behavior that harms the wider system.
Source links:
- The No-Stats All-Star: Michael Lewis's Profile of Shane Battier
25. Q: What is crowdsourcing, and how does it differ from relying on a small internal team?
A: Show answer
Crowdsourcing is the practice of obtaining ideas, information, judgments, designs, or other contributions from a broad or distributed group of people. It can reach contributors beyond the organization's normal boundaries and combine knowledge that is dispersed across professions, locations, or communities. A small internal team may have clearer accountability and easier access to confidential information, but it may lack the range of experience needed to solve an unfamiliar problem. Crowdsourcing can broaden the search for solutions, yet it also creates work in defining the task, managing submissions, protecting information, evaluating quality, and deciding who has authority to act. It should be selected because the problem benefits from distributed input, not simply because a large number of people can be contacted online.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an internal working group with an external crowd for a specific problem, including access, speed, confidentiality, and quality review."
Key point:
- Crowdsourcing expands the pool of potential contributors beyond a normal team.
- The organizer remains responsible for screening, validation, and implementation decisions.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
26. Q: When is crowdsourcing especially useful for a difficult problem?
A: Show answer
Crowdsourcing is useful when relevant knowledge is distributed across many people, when the problem has resisted conventional channels, or when a solution may require combinations of expertise that no single team possesses. It can help generate hypotheses, identify overlooked patterns, collect user experiences, test designs, or locate specialists with unusual knowledge. It is less suitable when the task requires immediate action, highly confidential information, tightly controlled professional judgment, or a decision that cannot be safely evaluated by the organizer. The chapter's examples include unresolved medical cases and an accessibility problem that attracted designers and engineers through an online post. Before launching a crowd, the problem owner should define the question, determine what information can be shared, establish review criteria, and plan how useful contributions will be tested.
Study instruction:
- "Select a stalled problem and assess whether it meets the conditions for crowdsourcing or should remain within a controlled expert process."
Key point:
- Crowdsourcing is valuable when knowledge is dispersed and conventional approaches have reached a limit.
- Confidentiality, urgency, safety, and the ability to validate suggestions determine whether it is appropriate.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
27. Q: How did CrowdMed apply crowdsourcing to unresolved medical diagnoses?
A: Show answer
CrowdMed allowed patients with difficult or unresolved medical problems to share their histories with a distributed group of case solvers, called detectives, who proposed possible explanations. The chapter emphasizes that the contributors were not limited to conventional medical specialists; some came from related or nonmedical fields and could notice patterns that a narrow group might overlook. The platform used vetting, moderation, incentives, and a process for ranking suggestions. An independent evaluation of 397 completed cases from 2013 to 2015 found that 59.6 percent of surveyed patients reported that the process provided insights that moved them closer to a correct diagnosis. That outcome was self-reported and did not establish that every suggestion was medically correct. The case illustrates crowdsourcing as a source of leads and perspectives, not a substitute for clinical diagnosis or treatment.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the CrowdMed process and distinguish patient-reported usefulness from independently confirmed diagnostic accuracy."
Key point:
- Distributed contributors can generate useful leads for difficult cases.
- Medical suggestions require qualified clinical assessment and follow-up validation.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
28. Q: Why can contributors from outside a profession help solve problems within that profession?
A: Show answer
People trained in the same profession may share useful standards and expertise, but they can also share the same blind spots or assumptions. Contributors from adjacent or unrelated fields may frame the problem differently, recognize a pattern that specialists overlook, or suggest a tool adapted from another domain. In the CrowdMed example, the chapter describes a deliberate inclusion of people who did not work or study in medicine. Their role was to widen the range of hypotheses rather than to replace licensed clinical judgment. This distinction matters: outsiders can contribute ideas, but the authority to diagnose, prescribe, certify, or approve must remain with qualified professionals. A good process welcomes relevant nontraditional input while setting clear boundaries for what contributors may decide and what requires expert verification.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one problem in your field that could benefit from adjacent expertise and specify which decisions must remain with qualified specialists."
Key point:
- Outsiders can reveal patterns or options that specialists may not consider.
- Nontraditional input must be separated from decisions that require professional authority.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
29. Q: What safeguards help maintain quality when a crowd contributes ideas or recommendations?
A: Show answer
A crowdsourcing process should define eligibility, explain the task precisely, provide enough context for meaningful contributions, and specify how submissions will be assessed. Moderators can remove irrelevant or unsafe material, while subject-matter experts can evaluate technical accuracy. Independent reviewers may compare suggestions, request supporting evidence, and identify conflicts of interest. The process should distinguish idea generation from final approval, retain an audit trail of submissions and decisions, and make clear that popularity is not proof of correctness. The CrowdMed example described in the chapter used vetting, case moderation, and incentives, while the independent evaluation stressed the need for further validation of outcomes. Quality controls should be proportionate to the consequences of error: medical, legal, safety, and financial recommendations require much stronger review than low-risk brainstorming.
Study instruction:
- "Design a review protocol that defines contributor eligibility, moderation, evidence requirements, expert approval, and an audit trail."
Key point:
- Crowdsourced content requires structured screening and validation.
- The more consequential the decision, the stronger the independent review must be.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
30. Q: How should reported success rates from a crowdsourcing platform be interpreted?
A: Show answer
Reported success figures should be examined for their definition of success, sample size, response rate, measurement method, and independence of verification. The chapter cites CrowdMed's own claim that many users later had their suggested diagnosis confirmed by a physician, but the independent academic evaluation used a more cautious outcome: 59.6 percent of surveyed patients said the process gave insights that moved them closer to the correct diagnosis. These are different measures and should not be treated as equivalent. A self-reported improvement in insight is not the same as a confirmed diagnosis, improved health outcome, or proven cost saving. When evaluating a platform, decision-makers should distinguish participant satisfaction, perceived usefulness, expert confirmation, and long-term outcome data. Claims should be no stronger than the evidence supports.
Study instruction:
- "For a crowdsourcing claim, identify whether the evidence measures satisfaction, perceived progress, verified accuracy, or a final outcome."
Key point:
- Different definitions of success produce different percentages and conclusions.
- Independent validation is necessary before treating platform claims as proof of effectiveness.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
31. Q: Why should crowdsourcing usually supplement rather than replace established expert channels?
A: Show answer
Established expert channels provide professional standards, accountability, access to regulated tools, and responsibility for decisions. Crowdsourcing can add ideas or identify overlooked possibilities, but it may also generate inaccurate, incomplete, or unsafe suggestions. The chapter's medical example is particularly clear: a crowd may help a patient identify a possible direction for investigation, but the diagnosis and treatment must be assessed by qualified clinicians. The same principle applies to engineering, finance, law, cybersecurity, and public safety. A crowd can widen the search, while accountable professionals evaluate the evidence and decide whether action is appropriate. The organizer should state this boundary before contributions are collected so that participants do not mistake suggestions, votes, or rankings for formal approval.
Study instruction:
- "Map which stages of a decision can use crowd input and which stages require formal professional authority."
Key point:
- Crowdsourcing can expand exploration without transferring professional accountability to the crowd.
- High-consequence decisions require qualified review and established approval procedures.
