- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
32. Q: How did Jimmy Choi's difficulty with a pill bottle lead to a crowdsourced design solution?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes how Jimmy Choi, an athlete living with early-onset Parkinson's disease, posted a video showing the difficulty of retrieving a small pill from a conventional container. The visible task attracted designers and engineers who proposed ways to make dispensing easier. Brian Alldridge, who had graphic-design experience but limited product-design experience, developed a concept that guided a pill into a small well and allowed it to be dispensed through a rotating tube. Other contributors with 3D-printing skills helped turn the concept into a working prototype and refine it so that pills were less likely to spill. The example shows how a clearly demonstrated user problem can attract a distributed group with complementary skills and how practical testing can improve an initial design.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the design sequence from observing the user's difficulty through concept development, prototyping, feedback, and refinement."
Key point:
- A concrete user problem can mobilize people with complementary skills.
- Iterative prototyping turns diverse suggestions into a testable solution.
33. Q: What made the pill-bottle example a form of user-centered and collaborative innovation?
A: Show answer
The project began with a real difficulty experienced by the intended user rather than with a designer's assumption about what users needed. The public video communicated the problem in a concrete way, allowing contributors to understand the physical challenge. The first design was not the finished solution: it had to be prototyped, tested, and refined by people with different capabilities. This process combined lived experience, visual communication, product thinking, and practical fabrication. User-centered innovation requires more than asking for feedback at the end; it involves understanding the context in which a product will be used and allowing that context to shape design decisions. The example also demonstrates that a contributor does not need a complete professional background in every relevant discipline if collaborators can supply missing expertise and test the result.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a product or service by observing an actual user performing the task and documenting the obstacles before proposing a redesign."
Key point:
- Direct observation of user difficulty can reveal design needs that assumptions overlook.
- Collaboration and prototyping allow different skills to improve a solution over successive iterations.
34. Q: How should a crowdsourcing project define its question and attract the right contributors?
A: Show answer
The organizer should first define the problem in a way that is specific enough to guide useful contributions but open enough to permit different approaches. The brief should explain the desired outcome, relevant constraints, available evidence, submission format, deadline, and evaluation criteria. It should identify which kinds of knowledge are required and where adjacent or unconventional expertise may add value. The organizer should also decide whether contributors need training, access to a prototype, or the ability to ask clarifying questions. A vague request tends to produce generic suggestions, while an overly narrow brief can exclude a promising alternative. The chapter's examples show that a clear demonstration of a problem can attract contributors who would not normally be part of the originating organization or profession.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a one-page crowdsourcing brief with a problem statement, constraints, requested contribution, eligibility, review criteria, and deadline."
Key point:
- A well-framed problem makes it easier for a diverse crowd to contribute relevant ideas.
- Contributor selection should reflect the knowledge gaps in the problem, not only existing professional boundaries.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
35. Q: What role do incentives and moderation play in a crowdsourcing system?
A: Show answer
Incentives can encourage people to contribute effort, share expertise, or improve submissions, while moderation helps maintain relevance and prevent harmful or misleading content from dominating. Rewards may be financial, reputational, educational, or social, but they should reinforce the behavior the project actually needs. Poorly designed incentives can encourage gaming, copying, excessive competition, or attention-seeking rather than careful problem-solving. Moderators should explain standards, remove abusive material, request clarification, and escalate submissions that raise safety or ethical concerns. Expert reviewers should assess high-risk recommendations independently of popularity or contributor reputation. The CrowdMed example described in the chapter used vetted contributors, case moderators, and rewards, illustrating how a platform can combine participation with structured quality controls.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the behavior an incentive is intended to encourage and the unintended behavior it might also reward."
Key point:
- Incentives can increase participation but can distort behavior if poorly aligned.
- Moderation and independent review protect the quality and safety of crowd contributions.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
36. Q: What risks should an organization assess before opening a problem to a crowd?
A: Show answer
Risks include disclosure of personal or commercially sensitive information, inaccurate advice, malicious submissions, conflicts of interest, intellectual-property disputes, unequal participation, bias in the evaluation process, and confusion about who is accountable for decisions. Crowdsourcing can also create reputational risk if contributors believe their ideas will be used without consent or if the organization ignores harmful consequences. Controls should include data minimization, informed consent, clear participation terms, access restrictions where necessary, moderation, documented evaluation criteria, conflict declarations, and an approval process proportionate to the risk. The organizer should explain how contributions may be used, whether they will be attributed, and whether compensation or ownership rights apply. A large crowd does not remove the organization's duty to protect people and make responsible decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Complete a risk assessment covering confidentiality, data protection, contributor rights, content quality, bias, and decision accountability."
Key point:
- Open participation creates governance and information risks that must be managed deliberately.
- The organization remains accountable for how contributions are used and for the decisions it makes.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
37. Q: What is the wisdom of crowds, and why can a group estimate be better than an individual estimate?
A: Show answer
The wisdom-of-crowds principle is that aggregating judgments from multiple people can produce an estimate that is more accurate than many individual estimates when participants contribute useful information and their errors are not all the same. If one person overestimates and another underestimates, aggregation can reduce the effect of individual error. The benefit depends on the structure of the crowd: participants need enough independence to avoid simply copying one another, and their judgments must be relevant to the question. A crowd that shares the same misinformation or follows a dominant voice can reproduce a common error. The principle therefore does not mean that majority opinion is always correct. It means that diverse, reasonably independent judgments can contain information that becomes clearer when combined appropriately.
Study instruction:
- "Design a small estimation exercise and compare the median or average of independent estimates with the individual estimates and the known answer."
Key point:
- Aggregation can reduce individual error when judgments contain sufficiently independent information.
- A large group is not wise merely because it is large or unanimous.
Source links:
- The Wisdom of the Inner Crowd in Three Large Natural Experiments
- Measuring the Crowd Within: Probabilistic Representations Within Individuals
38. Q: Why are independence and diversity important when aggregating judgments from a crowd?
A: Show answer
Diversity provides differences in knowledge, experience, and assumptions, while independence helps prevent one participant's opinion from simply reproducing another's. These properties are related but distinct. A group may be diverse in background yet become highly dependent if everyone receives the same initial estimate or defers to a senior member. Conversely, independent participants may still share similar experiences and make similar errors. Good aggregation requires a design that protects both the range of perspectives and the independence of initial judgments. For example, participants can submit estimates privately before a discussion, explain the evidence behind them, and revise only after considering new information. The organizer can then compare the distribution of answers and examine why estimates differ rather than relying solely on a vote.
Study instruction:
- "Review a group decision process and identify whether participants formed independent judgments or were influenced by a common anchor or authority."
Key point:
- Diversity broadens the information pool; independence reduces correlated errors.
- Private initial judgments can help preserve differences before group discussion.
Source links:
- The Wisdom of the Inner Crowd in Three Large Natural Experiments
- Measuring the Crowd Within: Probabilistic Representations Within Individuals
39. Q: What is an internal crowd, and how can a person create one without consulting other people?
A: Show answer
An internal crowd is a deliberate method of producing more than one perspective or judgment within the same person. The chapter explains that people can temporarily separate their first reaction from a second, more critical viewpoint by changing roles, imagining a disagreement, or asking what an informed critic would say. This does not create genuine independence because both perspectives still come from one person's knowledge and biases. However, it can reduce immediate attachment to the first answer and prompt reconsideration of assumptions. The method is useful when another person is unavailable or when a decision-maker wants to prepare before seeking external feedback. It should be treated as a structured self-review, not as a replacement for consultation when the problem requires expertise or independent judgment.
Study instruction:
- "Write an initial answer to a difficult question, then create a second answer from the perspective of a skeptical but constructive reviewer."
Key point:
- An internal crowd uses role-switching and deliberate reconsideration to generate alternative views.
- It can improve reflection but cannot fully remove the biases of a single person.
Source links:
- Measuring the Crowd Within: Probabilistic Representations Within Individuals
40. Q: How did the virtual-reality self-dialogue experiment illustrate perspective-taking?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a 2019 experiment in which participants discussed a personal sticking point while switching between an avatar resembling themselves and an avatar of Sigmund Freud. In the switching condition, participants effectively questioned themselves from another role and voice, creating psychological distance from the original problem. The chapter reports that participants in this condition were more likely than those receiving generic prompts to report behavioral change, partial problem resolution, and greater focus on solving the issue. The experiment illustrates how role-taking can make self-reflection feel like a conversation with another person. Its small sample and specific virtual-reality setup limit the strength of general conclusions. The practical lesson is to test perspective-taking as a reflective technique, not to assume that the experiment proves a clinical treatment effect.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the difference between generic self-reflection and role-switching, then identify a low-risk situation in which perspective-taking could be tried."
Key point:
- Adopting another role can create psychological distance from an emotionally charged problem.
- A small experimental result should not be treated as proof of a universal or clinical effect.
41. Q: What does the wisdom of inner crowds suggest about making the same estimate twice?
A: Show answer
Research on the wisdom of inner crowds suggests that a person can sometimes improve an estimate by producing a second judgment and combining it with the first. Vul and Pashler's 2008 study examined whether two estimates from one person could be aggregated in a way similar to combining judgments from different people. The chapter reports that the average of two estimates was more accurate than either estimate alone and that a delay between estimates could make the second judgment less dependent on the first. The method is most useful for uncertain estimates where there is no immediate objective answer available. It is not a license to average arbitrary opinions or to ignore new evidence. The person should record the initial estimate, deliberately reconsider the assumptions, and then compare the two judgments before combining them.
Study instruction:
- "Make two independent estimates of an uncertain quantity, record the assumptions behind each, and compare the combined estimate with the eventual result."
Key point:
- A second estimate can add information that improves an individual's judgment when it is not merely a repetition of the first.
- Deliberate separation and reconsideration are important to the method.
Source links:
- Measuring the Crowd Within: Probabilistic Representations Within Individuals
- The Wisdom of the Inner Crowd in Three Large Natural Experiments
42. Q: Why can a time delay improve the independence of a person's second estimate?
A: Show answer
A delay can reduce the immediate influence of the first answer by allowing some details of the original judgment to fade from working memory. When a person returns to the question later, they may approach it with a different set of cues or assumptions. The chapter describes a study in which estimates made weeks apart produced a more accurate combined result than estimates made immediately one after another. The important principle is not that a particular delay will always work, but that a second judgment should be generated with enough independence to contribute something new. A person can also increase independence by writing the first answer down, changing the framing of the question, or deliberately considering an alternative explanation before estimating again.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an immediate second estimate with one made after a planned delay and note which assumptions changed."
Key point:
- Time separation can reduce anchoring on the first response.
- The usefulness of a delay depends on whether the second judgment brings genuinely new reasoning.
Source links:
- Measuring the Crowd Within: Probabilistic Representations Within Individuals
- The Wisdom of the Inner Crowd in Three Large Natural Experiments
43. Q: How can a disagreement mindset improve a person's second judgment?
A: Show answer
A disagreement mindset asks a person to imagine that the second judgment comes from someone whose views differ from their own. Instead of merely repeating the first estimate, the person tries to identify why the original answer might be wrong and what a disagreeing observer would notice. A 2022 study found that combining an initial estimate with a second estimate made from the perspective of someone with whom the participant often disagreed could improve quantitative judgment. The chapter uses this idea to recommend deliberate consideration of the opposite view. The method works best when disagreement is used as a prompt for analysis rather than as an invitation to defend an opposite position regardless of evidence. The second estimate should be supported by alternative assumptions, not by contrarianism alone.
Study instruction:
- "For an important estimate, write the strongest plausible reason a thoughtful critic would disagree with your first answer and revise the estimate accordingly."
Key point:
- Imagining a disagreeing perspective can reveal assumptions hidden in an initial judgment.
- The goal is evidence-based reconsideration, not automatic opposition.
Source links:
- Taking a Disagreeing Perspective Improves the Accuracy of People's Quantitative Estimates
44. Q: What four-step process does the chapter recommend for reconsidering an initial estimate?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a practical sequence for creating a more independent second estimate. First, assume that the initial estimate may be wrong. Second, identify several assumptions or pieces of information that could have been mistaken. Third, determine what those alternatives imply and whether the original answer was likely too high or too low. Fourth, produce a new estimate based on the revised perspective. The process is useful because it turns vague doubt into a structured review of the reasoning behind the first answer. It can be adapted to non-numerical problems by asking what evidence would contradict the original view, what another person might notice, and what alternative explanation best fits the facts. The method should be documented so that the person can compare the logic of both judgments rather than relying on memory alone.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the four steps to a forecast or decision and record the assumptions changed between the first and second judgment."
Key point:
- A useful second estimate begins by challenging the assumptions behind the first.
- Structured reconsideration is more productive than simply guessing again.
Source links:
- Taking a Disagreeing Perspective Improves the Accuracy of People's Quantitative Estimates
- Measuring the Crowd Within: Probabilistic Representations Within Individuals
45. Q: Why is consulting another person generally more powerful than relying only on an internal crowd?
A: Show answer
Another person can contribute experiences, information, and assumptions that the decision-maker does not possess. Even after a delay, a person's second judgment may remain correlated with the first because both come from the same underlying knowledge and habits. A genuinely different person can introduce a more independent error pattern or reveal a factor the first person never considered. The chapter therefore presents internal self-questioning as a useful fallback rather than an equivalent replacement for external consultation. The best adviser is not necessarily the person with the highest status; the most useful contribution may come from someone who sees the problem differently and can explain why. For high-stakes decisions, external advice should be selected for relevant expertise and independence, and the final decision-maker should still evaluate the evidence and retain accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Compare your own second judgment with feedback from a person who has different experience and explain what new information the other person contributed."
Key point:
- External contributors can supply information and perspectives unavailable to one person.
- Internal reflection is useful, but it cannot fully reproduce independent outside judgment.
Source links:
- Measuring the Crowd Within: Probabilistic Representations Within Individuals
- The Wisdom of the Inner Crowd in Three Large Natural Experiments
46. Q: How can an individual use a devil's-advocate role without becoming needlessly negative?
A: Show answer
A devil's advocate should test assumptions and reveal weaknesses in reasoning, not oppose every idea for the sake of argument. The person can ask what evidence would disprove the current view, what alternative explanation fits the facts, which stakeholder might interpret the situation differently, and what could go wrong if the preferred plan is adopted. The exercise should be bounded by a clear question and should end with a revised assessment or a list of issues requiring further evidence. It is important to distinguish constructive challenge from cynicism, personal criticism, or endless debate. The chapter's internal-crowd approach uses perspective-taking to create distance from an initial reaction. In practice, the critic role is most useful when it improves the quality of a decision and then returns to a balanced evaluation of the options.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare three evidence-based questions a constructive critic would ask about a decision, then identify what would change your conclusion."
Key point:
- Devil's advocacy tests reasoning rather than attacking people or rejecting every proposal.
- The exercise should lead to better evidence, a revised view, or a clear decision about uncertainty.
Source links:
- Taking a Disagreeing Perspective Improves the Accuracy of People's Quantitative Estimates
47. Q: What does the fitness-equipment experiment suggest about the value of second ideas?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an experiment in which participants were asked to invent several pieces of fitness equipment. The inventors tended to believe that their first idea was the best, but a separate consumer panel often rated a later idea more highly. The later ideas were more abstract or surprising because the inventors had moved beyond their initial assumptions about what fitness equipment should look like. The lesson is that the first idea may be constrained by the most accessible examples or familiar categories. Asking for additional ideas can widen the search and create more unusual combinations. This does not mean that every second idea is better; the value lies in generating alternatives and evaluating them against user needs, feasibility, safety, and evidence rather than treating the first plausible answer as final.
Study instruction:
- "Generate at least three alternatives to a familiar design and evaluate them against user value, feasibility, and risk."
Key point:
- Additional ideas can move a person beyond the most familiar solution.
- Alternatives must be evaluated rather than accepted simply because they are newer.
Source links:
- Taking a Disagreeing Perspective Improves the Accuracy of People's Quantitative Estimates
48. Q: How should a team combine crowdsourcing with formal decision-making and accountability?
A: Show answer
A team should treat crowdsourcing as an input stage within a governed decision process. It can use a crowd to generate hypotheses, identify user needs, propose designs, or challenge assumptions. The organization should then classify submissions, check evidence, assess risks, obtain expert review, and compare viable options against agreed criteria. A named decision owner should document why a proposal was accepted, modified, tested, or rejected. For high-risk work, approval must remain within the formal authority structure, and the organization should preserve records of the evidence and controls used. This approach combines the breadth of distributed input with the reliability of accountable review. It also prevents a popular suggestion or highly rated submission from bypassing safety, legal, financial, or professional requirements.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a workflow showing where crowd input enters, who evaluates it, who approves action, and how the final decision is documented."
Key point:
- Crowdsourcing broadens idea generation but should not bypass governance or approval.
- A named decision owner must remain accountable for validation and implementation.

49. Q: How can an organization build a culture that repeatedly benefits from diverse input?
A: Show answer
Organizations can institutionalize diverse input by recruiting for complementary expertise, rotating selected collaborators, inviting cross-functional review, creating channels for user feedback, and making it safe to raise evidence-based concerns. Leaders should reward useful learning and constructive challenge rather than only agreement or short-term output. Teams should also document lessons from experiments so that insights survive staff changes and can be reused. The chapter's cases suggest that diversity is not a one-time hiring exercise: networks need renewal, and contributors need opportunities to influence the work. At the same time, organizations must preserve accountability, protect confidential information, and ensure that decision processes remain understandable. A sustainable culture combines open participation with clear standards for evidence, conduct, evaluation, and implementation.
Study instruction:
- "Assess whether your organization has recurring mechanisms for cross-functional input, external challenge, user participation, and documented learning."
Key point:
- Diverse input becomes a capability when it is built into recurring work practices.
- Openness must be paired with accountability, psychological safety, and knowledge retention.

50. Q: How can a team evaluate whether diversity or crowdsourcing actually improved a result?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should begin with a defined problem and a baseline, such as the previous solution's accuracy, cost, time, usability, or rate of failure. The team should record what new perspectives were introduced, which suggestions were tested, and why certain proposals were selected. It should compare outcomes using criteria established before the results are known, where possible, and distinguish improvements caused by the new input from changes in resources, staffing, or external conditions. Useful measures may include the number of distinct hypotheses, the proportion of suggestions that pass expert review, time to identify a viable solution, implementation cost, user satisfaction, error rates, and long-term performance. The team should also record negative results and unintended consequences. This prevents a successful anecdote from being treated as proof that the process always works.
Study instruction:
- "Create a before-and-after evaluation plan with a baseline, outcome measures, review criteria, and a record of rejected as well as adopted ideas."
Key point:
- The effectiveness of diverse input should be assessed against defined outcomes.
- A credible evaluation records both benefits and failures and avoids attributing every change to diversity alone.

51. Q: What are the main limitations of using an internal crowd to solve a difficult problem?
A: Show answer
An internal crowd remains one person's mind, so its perspectives are not fully independent. The same memories, assumptions, information gaps, and emotional reactions can influence both the first and second judgment. A person may also construct a weak opposing view or use self-questioning to reinforce a preferred answer. The method is less suitable when the decision requires specialized knowledge, independent verification, or a perspective outside the person's experience. It can also become unproductive if the person repeatedly revises an estimate without obtaining new evidence. The chapter presents internal crowds as a useful way to reconsider an initial judgment, particularly when external input is unavailable, but it also notes that a real crowd or another person generally contributes more independent information. The method should therefore be combined with evidence gathering when possible.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the knowledge and independence limits of a self-review before deciding whether outside advice or formal expert assessment is necessary."
Key point:
- A second internal perspective cannot fully escape the biases and information limits of the same person.
- Self-questioning is a supplement to independent evidence, not a substitute for it.
Source links:
- Measuring the Crowd Within: Probabilistic Representations Within Individuals
- The Wisdom of the Inner Crowd in Three Large Natural Experiments
52. Q: How should a decision-maker choose between an external crowd, a new collaborator, and an internal crowd?
A: Show answer
The choice depends on the nature of the knowledge gap, the consequence of error, the availability of contributors, and the sensitivity of the information. A new collaborator may be sufficient when the team needs one different discipline or an independent challenge. A broader crowd may be appropriate when useful knowledge is widely distributed or when many alternatives need to be generated. An internal crowd can help when the decision-maker is alone, needs to prepare before consultation, or wants to challenge an immediate emotional reaction. High-stakes decisions generally require qualified independent review regardless of which method generates the initial ideas. The chapter's examples suggest a progression from routine methods to outside perspectives and, when those are unavailable, structured self-questioning. The decision-maker should choose the smallest process that supplies the missing perspective without creating unnecessary risk or delay.
Study instruction:
- "For one difficult decision, compare the three methods by knowledge gap, urgency, confidentiality, expertise, and verification requirements."
Key point:
- Select the source of diversity according to the problem and the risk of error.
- Internal reflection, a new collaborator, and a broad crowd serve different purposes and are not interchangeable.
Source links:
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
- Measuring the Crowd Within: Probabilistic Representations Within Individuals
53. Q: How can a team use diversity to avoid becoming an echo chamber?
A: Show answer
An echo chamber develops when the same people repeatedly exchange and reinforce similar assumptions while alternative evidence or viewpoints receive little attention. A team can reduce this risk by inviting reviewers from different functions, consulting users or affected communities, rotating selected project roles, and deliberately seeking evidence that challenges the current plan. It should also distinguish genuine disagreement from personal conflict and ensure that minority views are recorded rather than silently discarded. The chapter's discussion of medical research and creative networks shows how fields can become intellectually narrow when the same groups repeatedly interact. However, simply adding a new person is not enough if the group ignores their contribution. The team needs a process that gives different evidence a fair hearing and explains how it affects the final decision.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recurring decision forum and identify how alternative evidence is invited, documented, discussed, and incorporated."
Key point:
- Echo chambers are reduced by meaningful exposure to different information and independent challenge.
- New perspectives matter only when the group has a process for hearing and evaluating them.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
- Gender-Diverse Teams Produce More Novel and Higher-Impact Scientific Ideas
54. Q: What practical sequence can an individual or organization follow when a problem remains stuck?
A: Show answer
Begin by defining the problem and recording what has already been tried, including the assumptions behind each attempt. Determine whether the current approach is repeating familiar patterns without producing new evidence. Identify the missing perspectives or knowledge, then decide whether to invite a new collaborator, assemble a diverse review group, or seek contributions from a broader crowd. Frame the question clearly and set criteria for evaluating suggestions. Generate alternatives before selecting a preferred option, and test promising ideas through a controlled experiment or prototype. For personal judgments, use a second estimate or a deliberate opposing perspective to challenge the first answer. Finally, document the evidence, decision, outcome, and lessons so that the process improves future work. The sequence combines the chapter's examples into a repeatable problem-solving discipline.
Study instruction:
- "Apply this sequence to a current unresolved issue and document the problem statement, missing perspectives, alternatives, test, decision, and learning."
Key point:
- Getting unstuck requires changing the information or process that produced the repeated result.
- Alternatives should be tested, evaluated, and documented before they become the new routine.
Source links:
- Networks, Creativity, and Time: Staying Creative through Brokerage and Network Rejuvenation
- Crowdsourcing Diagnosis for Patients With Undiagnosed Illnesses: An Evaluation of CrowdMed
55. Q: What are the most important lessons from the chapter on diversity and crowdsourcing?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that familiar habits and closed networks can trap individuals and organizations in repeated ways of thinking. New collaborators can bring nonredundant knowledge and disrupt routines, while diverse teams can broaden the questions and evidence considered. The Doctor Who and Pixar examples illustrate how network renewal and unconventional contributors can help established creative groups avoid complacency. The CrowdMed and Parkinson's pill-bottle examples show how a wider group can combine specialized and nontraditional skills to generate ideas or prototypes. When an external crowd is unavailable, the internal-crowd method offers a structured way to question an initial judgment through role-switching, delayed estimates, and consideration of the opposite view. The chapter's broader lesson is to seek genuinely different input when routine methods fail, then verify suggestions and retain responsibility for the final decision.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter using one example of network renewal, one example of crowdsourcing, and one example of an internal second perspective."
Key point:
- Different perspectives help people and teams discover options beyond established habits.
- Evidence, careful evaluation, and accountable implementation determine whether a new idea becomes a useful solution.

Table 10
Experimentation, Curiosity, and the Discipline of Implementation
1. Q: What is the central argument of the chapter on experimentation?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that people become unstuck when they stop treating familiar methods as unquestionable and begin testing alternatives. Experimentation helps identify which approach actually produces the desired result instead of relying on convention, habit, or intuition. It can involve a focused comparison of possible solutions to a specific problem, or a broader habit of treating life as a continuing experiment. The examples of Dave Berkoff, John Boyd, Steven Spurrier, Perpetual Guardian, and Max Deutsch show that curiosity and evidence can reveal opportunities that routine thinking misses. Experimentation generates options; implementation is still needed to make a breakthrough last.
Study instruction:
- Identify one persistent problem and write down the assumption or routine that could be tested rather than accepted.
Key point:
- Experimentation challenges defaults by comparing alternatives.
- Curiosity generates possibilities, while implementation turns a chosen option into results.
2. Q: What physical and motivational disadvantages did Dave Berkoff face as a collegiate backstroke swimmer?
A: Show answer
Compared with elite male swimmers, Berkoff was several inches shorter and about forty pounds lighter. He was not heavily recruited by leading swimming colleges and attended Harvard largely because of his academic record. He also disliked swimming practice and struggled to maintain motivation when he remained fifth or sixth among American backstrokers, outside the level needed to qualify for the Olympic team. His challenge was therefore not simply to train harder. He needed a method that could help him overcome both his physical disadvantage and his reluctance to repeat conventional training.
Study instruction:
- Separate a performance problem into constraints that can be changed through training and constraints that require a different strategy.
Key point:
- Berkoff faced physical, competitive, and motivational barriers.
- A breakthrough required a method suited to his actual circumstances.
3. Q: Why was Berkoff's curiosity more useful than simply copying the training habits of elite swimmers?
A: Show answer
Many elite swimmers relied on exceptional size, intense training schedules, and established techniques. Berkoff could not easily reproduce their natural physical advantages, and he disliked the repetitive practice they accepted. His curiosity led him to question whether the standard technique was the only route to speed. Instead of trying to become a slightly better version of the typical elite swimmer, he searched for a different source of advantage. The chapter presents this willingness to question convention as the beginning of his breakthrough.
Study instruction:
- Choose a field where you are not naturally advantaged and identify one accepted practice that might be open to experimentation.
Key point:
- Copying the dominant approach may preserve the disadvantage.
- Questioning the method can reveal a different route to performance.
4. Q: How did coach Joe Bernal use Berkoff's interest in experimentation to improve his motivation?
A: Show answer
Bernal recognized that Berkoff enjoyed learning new techniques but disliked ordinary practice. Rather than treating that dislike only as a discipline problem, Bernal introduced new methods at the end of long practice sessions. The coach and swimmer then refined the techniques through a series of small and moderate adjustments. This made training a testing ground for ideas instead of a purely repetitive obligation. Bernal's approach aligned the training process with Berkoff's curiosity, helping him persist long enough to discover a technical advantage.
Study instruction:
- Consider how a learning or training plan could be redesigned to connect repetitive practice with exploration and feedback.
Key point:
- Good coaching can adapt the learning process to a person's motivations.
- Experimentation made practice more engaging for Berkoff.
5. Q: What did Berkoff discover about the relative speed of swimming underwater?
A: Show answer
Berkoff calculated that swimmers move about 82 percent faster when their bodies are fully immersed than when they swim at the surface. Because competitive swimming is often decided by fractions of a second, the difference represented a major opportunity. Rather than accepting the conventional pattern of surfacing quickly, he reasoned that he should spend as much of the race underwater as rules and physiology allowed. The insight came from breaking the race into components and examining where speed could be gained, rather than assuming that the established technique was already optimal.
Study instruction:
- Break a performance task into stages and identify whether one stage offers a measurable advantage that conventional practice overlooks.
Key point:
- Small technical advantages can matter greatly in a sport decided by milliseconds.
- Analyze the components before accepting the standard method.
6. Q: Why was it difficult for Berkoff to remain underwater for longer periods?
A: Show answer
The human body naturally urges a swimmer to return to the surface because breathing is necessary and prolonged submersion creates discomfort. Berkoff's body initially forced him to surface after about fifteen meters. The technique therefore required more than an intellectual insight: he had to train his body to tolerate the urge to breathe while dolphin-kicking underwater. Over several months he extended his underwater distance to forty meters, roughly 80 percent of the first lap of an Olympic pool. The example shows that discovering a better method and developing the ability to execute it are separate challenges.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish between recognizing a promising technique and building the physical or practical capacity to use it reliably.
Key point:
- An idea is not a breakthrough until it can be executed.
- Berkoff had to train through discomfort to make the technique usable.
7. Q: What achievements established the effectiveness of the Berkoff Blastoff?
A: Show answer
Berkoff's breakout performance came at the 1987 NCAA Nationals, where he broke the NCAA record in the 100-yard backstroke. At the 1988 Olympic trials he broke the 100-meter world record twice. At the Seoul Olympics he won gold as part of the United States 4-by-100-meter medley relay, although Japan's Daichi Suzuki beat him in the individual 100-meter backstroke. The technique attracted attention because it allowed swimmers to cover a substantial part of the race underwater, where they moved faster than they did at the surface.
Study instruction:
- Trace how an experimental idea moved from practice to measurable competitive results in Berkoff's career.
Key point:
- The technique produced measurable records and Olympic success.
- Evidence from performance helped establish that the unconventional approach worked.
8. Q: How did Berkoff's technique influence other swimmers?
A: Show answer
Other swimmers observed the advantage of extended underwater swimming and began adopting the method. In the 1988 Olympic 100-meter backstroke final, several competitors remained underwater well beyond the conventional short start. Daichi Suzuki had learned Berkoff's technique from grainy video footage and used a similar approach to win the individual race. This illustrates how an innovation can spread beyond its originator once its value becomes visible. It also shows that a breakthrough may change the behavior of competitors even before an institution formally recognizes or regulates it.
Study instruction:
- Identify how a successful experiment in your field could be observed, copied, adapted, or improved by competitors.
Key point:
- A demonstrated advantage can spread quickly.
- Innovation often changes the practices of others before formal rules catch up.
9. Q: Why did swimming's governing body restrict underwater backstroke after the 1988 Olympics?
A: Show answer
The effectiveness of the Berkoff Blastoff prompted swimming's governing body, FINA, to add a rule requiring some part of a swimmer to break the water's surface throughout the race. Swimmers could remain fully submerged only during the turn and for a limited distance after the start and each turn. The permitted distance was initially ten meters and later increased to fifteen meters. The chapter notes that FINA cited safety concerns for young swimmers and argued that the technique departed from the traditional understanding of backstroke. The rule changed the competitive environment in which Berkoff's innovation had flourished.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a situation in which a new technique challenges existing rules and distinguish concerns about safety from resistance to change.
Key point:
- Innovation can provoke institutional limits.
- New methods must be evaluated against both performance and legitimate safety requirements.
10. Q: What does Berkoff's disagreement with FINA reveal about innovation and tradition?
A: Show answer
Berkoff believed that swimming should reward both speed and ingenuity. He felt that restricting underwater swimming privileged the natural advantages of unusually tall, powerful athletes over the earned advantage created through experimentation. The chapter presents this as a tension between established standards and alternative routes to excellence. Berkoff did not argue that every innovation should be accepted without limits; rather, he objected to a rule that, in his view, suppressed a legitimate technique. The example invites readers to examine whether a tradition protects essential values or merely preserves familiar ways of doing things.
Study instruction:
- Review a rule or convention in your field and ask what purpose it serves, whom it benefits, and whether it blocks legitimate innovation.
Key point:
- Tradition can preserve useful standards but can also restrict earned innovation.
- Examine the reason behind a rule before accepting it as permanent.
11. Q: Why does the chapter argue that experimentation is especially available outside tightly regulated sports?
A: Show answer
In swimming, formal rules constrain how a competitor may perform, and FINA could restrict the distance swimmers remained underwater. In fields such as art, music, business, writing, parenting, and relationships, there are often fewer fixed rules governing the method. People can therefore test alternatives more freely. The chapter argues that many familiar practices in everyday life are conventions rather than necessities. When a person becomes stuck, questioning those conventions may reveal a better approach without violating any essential requirement.
Study instruction:
- List three conventions in your work or personal life and determine which could be tested safely without violating an important requirement.
Key point:
- Many everyday practices are conventions rather than fixed laws.
- Where rules permit, experimentation can reveal better methods.
12. Q: Who was Colonel John Boyd, and what made him an experimental thinker?
A: Show answer
John Boyd was a highly skilled United States fighter pilot and instructor who excelled at simulated air-to-air combat. He defeated most students quickly and was known for his speed and maneuvering ability. Despite these natural gifts, he continued to question established methods and sought to record, test, and share better strategies. His engineering education reinforced his interest in systematic analysis. Boyd's importance in the chapter is not only his flying ability but also his effort to turn repeated observation and experimentation into a method that could be taught and applied beyond combat.
Study instruction:
- Identify a high performer who continues to question established methods and examine how that habit contributes to improvement.
Key point:
- Natural ability does not remove the need to experiment.
- Boyd used analysis and testing to improve methods and teach others.
13. Q: What was Boyd's Aerial Attack Study, and why did it become influential?
A: Show answer
After completing an engineering degree, Boyd began developing a tactical manual for the United States Air Force in 1959. He spent about a month creating an outline and then dictated his ideas into a tape recorder, producing a 150-page document titled Aerial Attack Study. The manual became widely sought after by pilots and instructors, and Boyd's first printing of six hundred copies was quickly taken. Its influence came from translating practical experience and systematic analysis into a resource that others could use to improve their own decisions and tactics.
Study instruction:
- Consider how a useful method developed through experience could be documented so that others can test and apply it.
Key point:
- Boyd converted experience and analysis into a transferable method.
- Documenting tested ideas can spread improvements beyond the original practitioner.
14. Q: What does the OODA acronym stand for?
A: Show answer
OODA stands for observe, orient, decide, and act. Boyd developed the loop to help fighter pilots respond more effectively during rapidly changing air-to-air combat. The first stage gathers an accurate picture of the situation. The second interprets that information in light of the decision-maker's background and circumstances. The third selects an approach, and the fourth puts it into practice. The model is called a loop because new events can change the situation, requiring the person to observe again and revise the plan. The chapter applies the same logic to overcoming sticking points in ordinary life.
Study instruction:
- Write the four OODA stages in order and apply each one to a current problem.
Key point:
- OODA means observe, orient, decide, and act.
- The stages form a repeating cycle rather than a one-time checklist.
15. Q: What is the purpose of the observe stage in the OODA loop?
A: Show answer
Observe means gathering information and identifying the situation as accurately as possible before choosing a response. Berkoff's observations included recognizing that he avoided conventional practice, that he was smaller than many competitors, and that his current ranking was not sufficient for Olympic qualification. Without an accurate account of the problem, a person may work harder on the wrong issue or overlook a constraint that requires a different strategy. Observation should therefore include relevant facts, current performance, obstacles, and the conditions under which the problem occurs.
Study instruction:
- Describe a sticking point using observable facts and separate those facts from assumptions about why the problem exists.
Key point:
- Accurate observation is the foundation of effective action.
- A person cannot solve a problem well if the real constraints remain unidentified.
16. Q: Why did Boyd consider the orient stage especially important?
A: Show answer
Orient involves interpreting the observed situation through the decision-maker's accumulated experiences and characteristics. Boyd believed it was probably the most important stage because the same information can suggest different actions to different people. Orientation is shaped by cultural beliefs, attitudes, personality, education, genetic heritage, and other influences. Berkoff's intelligence, curiosity, education, and limited natural physical advantages made experimentation a particularly suitable strategy for him. His background helped him see training as an opportunity to test techniques rather than simply repeat conventional routines.
Study instruction:
- Reflect on how your background, values, strengths, and limitations influence the solutions you consider first.
Key point:
- Orientation connects facts to the decision-maker's particular circumstances.
- A useful strategy should fit the person and the problem.
17. Q: What happens during the decide stage of the OODA loop?
A: Show answer
Decide means selecting an action after examining the available information and possible approaches. Berkoff used his observations about underwater speed to identify a promising opportunity: spending more of the race beneath the surface. The decision stage turns analysis into a choice that can be tested. It does not require absolute certainty, because the loop allows the person to observe again and revise the decision when new evidence appears. A sound decision should be specific enough to guide action while remaining open to correction.
Study instruction:
- List at least two possible responses to a problem, compare them against the evidence, and state which one you will test first.
Key point:
- Deciding converts analysis into a chosen direction.
- The choice should be testable and revisable as new information appears.
18. Q: What does the act stage require after a decision has been made?
A: Show answer
Act means turning the decision into a concrete plan and carrying it out. Berkoff worked with coach Joe Bernal to develop an underwater-training plan and gradually increase the distance he could swim before surfacing. The act stage therefore involves more than expressing an intention; it requires specific steps, practice, and follow-through. The results of action then become new information for the next observation stage. In this way, implementation is both the execution of a decision and a source of evidence about whether the decision was sound.
Study instruction:
- Convert a decision into a short action plan with a first step, a practice schedule, and a way to measure results.
Key point:
- Action makes a decision real and produces evidence for the next cycle.
- A plan needs execution, not just agreement.
19. Q: Why is the OODA model described as a loop rather than a linear sequence?
A: Show answer
Situations change after a person begins acting. Boyd used the example of a pilot whose view changes when clouds clear and the aircraft faces the sun. A plan that was appropriate a moment earlier may no longer fit the new conditions. The pilot must observe again, reinterpret the situation, and adjust the response. Berkoff faced a similar change when FINA restricted his underwater technique. He could not simply continue with the original plan; he had to reassess the rules and find another route to success. The loop makes adaptation part of the method.
Study instruction:
- Identify a recent situation in which new information made your original plan less suitable and describe how you adjusted it.
Key point:
- OODA is iterative because conditions change.
- Reassessment and adaptation are essential parts of effective problem-solving.
20. Q: How did Berkoff use the OODA loop again after FINA restricted his underwater technique?
A: Show answer
After the rule change, Berkoff initially retired but later returned to pursue another Olympic medal in 1992. He went back to coach Dick Shoulberg and relearned conventional backstroke. He also used overdistance training, swimming farther than the target race distance, and made use of his flexibility and relatively small size to turn more tightly at the wall. This was a second cycle of observation, orientation, decision, and action. Berkoff adapted to a changed environment rather than assuming that the original breakthrough was the only path available.
Study instruction:
- Choose a plan that has been blocked by changed conditions and identify a second strategy that uses your remaining strengths.
Key point:
- A breakthrough method may become unavailable.
- The OODA loop helps people reassess and find another viable route.
21. Q: What medals did Berkoff win after returning to competition for the 1992 Olympics?
A: Show answer
At the 1992 Barcelona Olympics, Berkoff won a gold medal in the 4-by-100-meter medley relay and a bronze medal in the individual 100-meter backstroke. These results followed his return to conventional backstroke after the underwater technique was restricted. His performance demonstrated that the earlier innovation was not the only way he could compete successfully. He reassessed his strengths, adopted a different coach and training approach, and implemented a new plan. The episode reinforces the value of adapting when circumstances change rather than becoming attached to one solution.
Study instruction:
- Identify which strengths remain available when an original method is no longer permitted or practical.
Key point:
- Adaptation can preserve progress after a preferred method disappears.
- Reassess strengths and build a new implementation plan.
22. Q: How does the chapter connect the OODA loop with experimentation?
A: Show answer
Experimentation is central to the observe and orient stages because people need to collect information and understand which options may work. Observation identifies the problem and gathers evidence. Orientation interprets that evidence through the person's abilities, experience, and circumstances. Decisions and actions then test the chosen approach, and the resulting experience informs the next cycle. Boyd's engineering perspective led him to believe that progress requires understanding available options and testing which ones produce the desired outcomes. The loop therefore combines curiosity, analysis, choice, action, and revision.
Study instruction:
- Use the OODA stages to plan a small experiment and specify what evidence will cause you to revise the approach.
Key point:
- Experimentation supplies evidence for the OODA loop.
- Repeated observation and adjustment make improvement responsive to real conditions.
23. Q: What is experimentalism, and how does it help people choose a better path?
A: Show answer
Experimentalism is the belief that comparing two or more alternatives helps reveal which path is most effective. Rather than relying entirely on intuition or convention, a person tests options and observes the results. The chapter identifies two reasons to collect data: to determine which approach works best among several possibilities, and to persuade others when one already believes a particular approach is superior. The method makes decisions more transparent because the evidence can be examined rather than relying only on status, tradition, or personal confidence.
Study instruction:
- Identify two plausible ways to solve a problem and define a fair comparison that would help you decide between them.
Key point:
- Compare alternatives rather than relying only on instinct.
- Data can guide a choice and help communicate why it is preferable.
24. Q: What were the two purposes of collecting data through experiments in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies two purposes. The first is to determine which approach is best when several alternatives are available. The second is to persuade other people that a preferred approach is superior when the experimenter already has a view. In both cases, data can replace unsupported claims with observable results. The distinction matters because experimentation is not only a private decision-making tool; it can also help organizations and communities reconsider practices that are defended by tradition. A well-designed test can reveal whether a familiar belief still matches current conditions.
Study instruction:
- Decide whether an experiment is intended to choose among alternatives or to test a claim you want others to reconsider.
Key point:
- Experiments can select the best option or provide evidence for changing others' beliefs.
- Clarify the purpose before testing.
25. Q: What problem was Steven Spurrier trying to solve with the 1976 Judgment of Paris?
A: Show answer
Steven Spurrier was an English wine merchant who operated a wine shop and wine school in Paris, where French, Italian, and Spanish wines dominated the market. He found it difficult to distinguish his business from other merchants selling similar Old World wines. He also believed that Californian wines were as good as the traditional French labels but knew that Parisian buyers were skeptical. He therefore designed a public blind-tasting event that could both promote his business and test the assumption that French wines were inherently superior.
Study instruction:
- Identify a widely accepted quality ranking in your field and consider how a fair comparison could test whether it remains justified.
Key point:
- Spurrier used an experiment to challenge an entrenched belief.
- The experiment also created a distinctive opportunity for his business.
26. Q: How was the Judgment of Paris tasting designed?
A: Show answer
Spurrier arranged a blind tasting involving ten white wines and ten red wines. Six wines in each category came from California, while four came from France. He invited nine distinguished French wine experts to taste and rate the wines and to judge whether each seemed French or American. The blind format concealed the labels, reducing the opportunity for judges to rely on the reputation or origin of a bottle. The experiment was designed to compare the wines themselves rather than the assumptions attached to their country of origin.
Study instruction:
- Design a blind comparison for a product or service where brand reputation might influence people's judgments.
Key point:
- Blind testing reduces the influence of labels and reputation.
- A fair comparison focuses attention on the qualities being evaluated.
27. Q: What happened during the Judgment of Paris, and why was the result surprising?
A: Show answer
The judges began with a strong belief that good wine should be French, and some even misidentified French wines as Californian and Californian wines as French. The two unanimous winners were a 1973 chardonnay from Chateau Montelena and a 1973 cabernet sauvignon from Stag's Leap Wine Cellars, both from California's Napa Valley. The result challenged the assumption that French wines were automatically superior. It forced wine experts and consumers to reconsider the quality of New World producers and helped Californian wineries attract wider attention.
Study instruction:
- Compare an established belief with the results of a test and explain what the evidence does and does not prove.
Key point:
- The blind tasting showed that Californian wines could compete with leading French wines.
- Evidence can challenge inherited reputations.
28. Q: Why was the blind format important to the impact of the Judgment of Paris?
A: Show answer
Blind tasting removed the labels that had shaped the judges' expectations. Without knowing the country or producer, judges had to assess the wine based on the experience of tasting it. This made it harder to apply the simple rule that a good wine must be French. The chapter describes the experiment as a way to strip away assumptions and reveal evidence that the established hierarchy had obscured. The lesson extends beyond wine: when reputation, prestige, or familiarity may bias judgment, hiding those cues can produce a fairer comparison.
Study instruction:
- Identify labels, titles, or reputational cues that may influence a decision and consider how to conceal them during evaluation.
Key point:
- Blind evaluation can reduce status and reputation bias.
- Remove irrelevant cues when testing the underlying quality of alternatives.
29. Q: How did the Judgment of Paris change perceptions of Californian wine?
A: Show answer
The tasting did not cause French wine enthusiasts to switch to Californian wine overnight, but it began to change how the wine world judged New World producers. The results gave Californian wineries credible evidence that their products could compete with respected French labels. The chapter notes that Chateau Montelena and Stag's Leap Wine Cellars soon received increased attention and commercial interest. The experiment therefore had two effects: it demonstrated that Californian wines could perform well, and it persuaded people to give them a more serious second look.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a credible test result could change the reputation of a product or organization over time rather than immediately.
Key point:
- Experiments can change the standards by which people evaluate alternatives.
- Reputation shifts as evidence accumulates and spreads.
30. Q: Why does the chapter question the conventional five-day, eight-hour workweek?
A: Show answer
The chapter points out that work schedules have changed substantially over time. In the eighteenth century, many Americans worked six twelve-hour days, while average weekly hours gradually fell through industrialization and the Great Depression. The forty-hour workweek became established in the 1930s and remained common for almost a century despite major changes in technology and working life. The author asks why this arrangement should be treated as optimal simply because it is familiar. The example illustrates how a historical convention can become an unquestioned default even when conditions have changed.
Study instruction:
- Choose a longstanding schedule or process and ask whether its original assumptions still apply to current conditions.
Key point:
- Familiar work patterns are not necessarily optimal.
- Historical conventions can be tested rather than accepted as permanent.
31. Q: What did Perpetual Guardian test in its 2018 four-day workweek trial?
A: Show answer
Perpetual Guardian, an estate-planning firm in New Zealand, conducted a six-week trial in 2018 involving about 240 employees. Staff nominated a day off each week while retaining their existing salaries. Founder Andrew Barnes believed that the four-day structure could preserve productivity and customer service while improving employees' lives. The company worked with two universities to monitor productivity and staff satisfaction. The trial treated the schedule as a testable business arrangement rather than assuming that fewer workdays must mean less output.
Study instruction:
- Design a limited workplace trial that changes one scheduling assumption while keeping the main performance measures visible.
Key point:
- Perpetual Guardian tested a four-day week with unchanged salaries.
- A time-limited field trial can test assumptions in real operating conditions.
32. Q: What results did Perpetual Guardian report from its four-day workweek experiment?
A: Show answer
Employees reported having more time for family, hobbies, household tasks, and personal responsibilities. The company found that workers were more efficient, more engaged with customers, less stressed, and more satisfied with work-life balance. Revenue remained stable while costs declined because the company's power bill fell. These results persuaded the company to make the policy permanent. The example shows why field experiments can be persuasive: they measure how a proposed change affects both employees and organizational outcomes rather than relying only on theoretical arguments.
Study instruction:
- Select employee and organizational indicators that would allow a workplace experiment to evaluate both well-being and performance.
Key point:
- The trial reported improvements in efficiency and well-being while revenue remained stable.
- Measure multiple outcomes, not only hours worked.
33. Q: What limitations should be considered when interpreting the four-day workweek experiment?
A: Show answer
The chapter cautions that the four-day structure may not work in every workplace, industry, or country. It also raises the possibility of a Hawthorne effect, in which workers change their behavior because they know conditions have been altered and their performance is being observed. That effect may contribute to improved output or self-reported satisfaction. The author argues that it is unlikely to explain all the reported benefits because Perpetual Guardian maintained the policy over several years and similar outcomes were reported elsewhere. The case supports experimentation, not a universal claim that every organization should adopt the same schedule.
Study instruction:
- List possible confounding factors in a workplace trial and decide how a longer observation period or comparison group could address them.
Key point:
- Positive trial results require context and replication.
- A successful experiment in one setting does not prove universal suitability.
34. Q: What other examples does the chapter cite to show that shorter workweeks can be tested in different settings?
A: Show answer
The chapter mentions shorter-workweek experiments involving about one percent of Iceland's workforce between 2015 and 2019, Microsoft Japan in 2019, civil service workers in The Gambia between 2013 and 2017, and companies in the United Kingdom between 2018 and 2019. These examples were reported to show similar benefits for well-being, productivity, or efficiency. The range of settings strengthens the case that the idea deserved serious examination, although the chapter does not claim that every context produced identical results or that one schedule fits every employer.
Study instruction:
- Compare workplace experiments from different settings and identify which conditions might explain differences in their outcomes.
Key point:
- Replication across settings can strengthen confidence in an idea.
- Context still matters when deciding whether to adopt a change.
35. Q: Why does the chapter consider real-world field experiments more persuasive than theoretical proposals alone?
A: Show answer
A theoretical paper can explain why a change might work, but a field experiment shows how it performs under actual conditions. Spurrier's blind tasting challenged assumptions about wine through direct comparison, while Perpetual Guardian measured a new work schedule in a functioning business. These tests gave people concrete evidence that could be observed and discussed. The chapter argues that experiments can move public or organizational opinion because they demonstrate results rather than merely asking people to accept an argument. A good field test can therefore change both a decision and the assumptions behind it.
Study instruction:
- Identify a debate in your organization where a small field experiment could provide more useful evidence than another discussion or presentation.
Key point:
- Field experiments make claims observable in real conditions.
- Demonstrated results can persuade people more effectively than abstract arguments alone.
36. Q: What are the two broad forms of experimentation described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The first form is focused experimentation: comparing possible solutions to a specific problem, as Berkoff did with swimming techniques, Spurrier did with wine, and Perpetual Guardian did with work schedules. The second form is global curiosity, treating life as a continuing experiment by exploring unfamiliar skills, asking questions, and testing assumptions across different domains. Focused experiments help solve a defined problem. Global curiosity increases the chance of discovering unexpected interests and opportunities before a person becomes stuck. The two forms complement each other because broad exploration can generate ideas for later focused tests.
Study instruction:
- Identify one current problem suitable for a focused experiment and one unfamiliar area you could explore for broader learning.
Key point:
- Focused tests compare solutions to a specific problem.
- Global curiosity expands the range of ideas and experiences available.
37. Q: How does the chapter compare children's curiosity with adult curiosity?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites an estimate that five-year-old children ask between two hundred and three hundred questions each day, while adults ask about twenty or thirty. It suggests that people lose roughly 90 percent of their natural curiosity as they grow older. The contrast is used to explain why adults may accept conventions and defaults that children would question. Curiosity is important because it encourages exploration and idea generation, both of which support creativity and help people discover alternatives when familiar methods fail.
Study instruction:
- Keep a record of the questions you ask during one day and identify which assumptions you accepted without examination.
Key point:
- Curiosity tends to diminish with age, but it can be deliberately cultivated.
- Asking more questions expands the search for alternatives.
38. Q: What was Max Deutsch's Month to Master project?
A: Show answer
Max Deutsch created a project in which he attempted to master twelve new skills, one each month. The challenges covered physical, artistic, and intellectual activities, from memorizing a deck of cards and drawing portraits to performing a backflip, playing guitar, freestyle rapping, and completing difficult crossword puzzles. He documented daily progress and setbacks rather than presenting learning as an effortless success story. The project demonstrated how structured curiosity can turn unfamiliar activities into manageable experiments, allowing a person to learn about both a skill and their own interests.
Study instruction:
- Choose a skill you have never tried and define a one-month experiment with a clear, measurable outcome.
Key point:
- Structured curiosity turns unfamiliar pursuits into experiments.
- Documenting setbacks as well as progress makes learning more informative.
39. Q: What does Max Deutsch's card-memorization challenge reveal about experimentation and limited time?
A: Show answer
Deutsch taught himself to memorize the order of a deck of cards in under two minutes. He reached a time of one minute and forty-seven seconds near the end of November 2016, despite having a busy work schedule. He said that some days he trained for only forty-five minutes and often practiced during his Caltrain commute. The example shows that a focused experiment does not always require abandoning other responsibilities. A clear target, repeated practice, and willingness to test methods can produce meaningful progress within limited time.
Study instruction:
- Design a short daily practice routine that fits an existing schedule and define a result that can be measured at the end of the month.
Key point:
- Focused experiments can fit into limited time.
- A clear target and consistent testing can make learning more manageable.
40. Q: How did Deutsch use experimentation to develop skills beyond memorization?
A: Show answer
After memorizing cards, Deutsch moved to drawing realistic self-portraits and then to a wide range of other challenges. He studied drawing through a structured course, spent eight hours on a portrait that eventually came together on Christmas Day, and later experimented with physical, artistic, and intellectual tasks. The variety mattered because each challenge exposed him to a different way of learning and a different possible identity. The project was not limited to perfecting one ability; it was a way to discover what he enjoyed and what he could become.
Study instruction:
- Choose a new skill outside your usual domain and identify what it might teach you about your interests or learning style.
Key point:
- Exploring different domains can reveal new capabilities and interests.
- Curiosity can be valuable even when a skill is not pursued permanently.
41. Q: Why was Deutsch's challenge to defeat chess champion Magnus Carlsen especially difficult?
A: Show answer
Deutsch's twelfth challenge was to defeat a computer program modeled on Magnus Carlsen's playing style. Carlsen had become a grandmaster at thirteen and held the world's top ranking in several chess variants. Deutsch was not a strong chess player, so he used a crash course to recognize common patterns and positions. Carlsen agreed to meet him in Hamburg in 2017 after the project attracted a large audience. Deutsch made a small but fatal mistake on his ninth move, and the game ended after thirty-nine moves. The attempt was impressive given the gap in experience.
Study instruction:
- Select a stretch goal that is far beyond your current ability and identify a realistic learning experiment that could prepare you for it.
Key point:
- An ambitious challenge can expose learning opportunities even when success is unlikely.
- Preparation and experimentation still produce useful progress.
42. Q: What did Deutsch gain from the chess challenge even though he did not defeat Carlsen?
A: Show answer
Deutsch did not beat Carlsen, but he learned to recognize chess patterns, attracted a large audience to his project, and earned Carlsen's respect for his effort. Carlsen agreed to a rematch after Deutsch had more time to prepare. The chapter emphasizes that the value of the challenge was broader than winning. Deutsch learned about his ability to take on unfamiliar tasks, and the experience contributed to the direction of his later work. Experiments can produce information, relationships, and opportunities even when the original target is not achieved.
Study instruction:
- After a difficult project, review what you learned, whom you met, and which new opportunities emerged in addition to the main result.
Key point:
- An experiment can be valuable without achieving its headline goal.
- Learning and unexpected opportunities are also meaningful outcomes.
43. Q: How did the Month to Master project influence Deutsch's later career?
A: Show answer
Deutsch concluded that he enjoyed combining the intrinsic rewards of learning with the external rewards of earning a living. His experiments helped him explore possible identities and recognize an interest in entrepreneurship. He later founded Monthly, an online business that pairs people with experts who teach creative skills over a month, including music production, painting, singing, and filmmaking. The chapter traces the origins of this venture back to a childhood habit of exploring many interests. The project shows how a sequence of small experiments can reveal a broader direction for a career.
Study instruction:
- Review several past projects and identify the activities that repeatedly generated both learning and sustained interest.
Key point:
- Experimentation can reveal career preferences.
- A series of small explorations may clarify a larger direction.
44. Q: How can asking 'Why?' and 'Why not?' help adults become more curious?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends questioning conventions that people usually accept without reflection. Deutsch's friend, for example, questioned why lunch meetings had to begin on the hour and chose less conventional times to avoid predictable crowds. The point is not that every convention should be broken, but that asking why it exists can reveal whether it serves a useful purpose. Adults often follow routines because they are familiar, not because they have been tested. Deliberate questioning can uncover small opportunities for improvement and encourage broader curiosity.
Study instruction:
- Choose a routine convention and ask what problem it originally solved and whether it remains useful today.
Key point:
- Ask why a convention exists before accepting it.
- Some defaults persist through habit rather than demonstrated value.
45. Q: Why does the chapter recommend browsing rather than relying exclusively on searching?
A: Show answer
Searching requires a person to have a rough goal or topic in mind, which encourages deeper exploration of known interests. Browsing exposes people to topics and ideas they did not know to seek. The chapter contrasts digital search habits with the chance encounters that occur while walking through a physical library or bookstore. Browsing can broaden the contours of a person's knowledge and create unexpected connections. Search is valuable for focused questions, but browsing is useful when the goal is to discover unfamiliar ideas and expand the range of possible interests.
Study instruction:
- Spend time browsing a library, bookstore, or unfamiliar topic area and record three ideas you would not have searched for directly.
Key point:
- Searching deepens known interests, while browsing increases exposure to unfamiliar topics.
- Both are useful for different purposes.
46. Q: How can keeping a list of puzzling facts or questions cultivate curiosity?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends maintaining an ongoing list of facts, ideas, and experiences that are confusing or surprising. Instead of allowing a question to pass unnoticed, a person records it and returns to investigate it. The author recalls a childhood list of countries, capitals, and obscure words on the family refrigerator. The list broadened knowledge and encouraged a habit of exploration. A curiosity list makes uncertainty visible and turns small moments of confusion into opportunities for learning.
Study instruction:
- Start a list of unanswered questions and add to it whenever something in daily life surprises or puzzles you.
Key point:
- A curiosity list preserves questions that might otherwise disappear.
- Recording uncertainty can build a lasting habit of exploration.
47. Q: What is idea linking, and how does curiosity support it?
A: Show answer
Idea linking is the tendency to move from one concept to another, creating a growing network of connected ideas.
